Dual Induction CLT for High-dimensional m m -dependent Data
Heejong Bong, Arun Kumar Kuchibhotla, Alessandro Rinaldo

Abstract
We derive novel and sharp high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for the sum of m m -dependent random vectors over the class of hyper-rectangles exhibiting only a poly-logarithmic dependence in the dimension. Our results hold under minimal assumptions, such as non-degenerate covariances and finite third moments, and yield a sample complexity of order m / n \sqrt{m/n} , aside from logarithmic terms, matching the optimal rates established in the univariate case. When specialized to the sums of independent non-degenerate random vectors, we obtain sharp rates under the weakest possible conditions. On the technical side, we develop an inductive relationship between anti-concentration inequalities and Berry–Esseen bounds, inspired by the classical Lindeberg swapping method and the concentration inequality approach for dependent data, that may be of independent interest.

1
Introduction

The accuracy of the central limit theorem in growing and even in infinite dimensions is a classic topic in probability theory that has been extensively studied since at least the 1950’s; see, e.g.,
Paulauskas and Račkauskas 2012
and
Sazonov 1981
for historical accounts of the central limit theorem in its various forms. In a seminal contribution,
Bentkus 2003
derived a high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for normalized sums of
n
independent and isotropic
p
-dimensional random vectors over appropriate classes of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. In details, if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d. centered random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with identity covariance matrix and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are independent
p
-dimensional centered standard Gaussian random vectors,
Bentkus 2003
proved that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\in A\right)\right|\preceq\frac{C_{d}(\mathcal{A})\mathbb{E}\|X_{1}\|^{3}}{\sqrt{n}},

where
\mathcal{A}
is a class subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
satisfying mild regularity conditions,
C_{d}(\mathcal{A})
is its Gaussian isoperimetric constant and
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean norm. The bound is at least of order
p^{3/2}n^{-1/2}
(e.g. when
\mathcal{A}
consists of all Euclidean balls) and is of order at least
p^{7/4}n^{-1/2}
for the larger class of convex sets. While, in principle, this result allows for applications of the central limit theorem in growing dimensions, its usefulness is limited to settings in which the dimension
p
is of smaller order than the sample size
n
.

The class of hyper-rectangles – and, more generally, of polyhedra with small enough combinatorial complexity and of sparsely convex sets
(see
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017a
, for details)
– are not covered by Bentkus’ result. Yet, remarkably, they admit high-dimensional Berry-Eseen bounds with only a poly-logarithmically dependence on the dimension.
Results of this type, initially put forward by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2013
, demonstrate that formal statistical inference via Gaussian approximations is feasible even when the dimension is much larger than the sample size; see, e.g.,
Belloni et al. 2018
for a selection of notable applications in high-dimensional and non-parametric statistics. Largely motivated by the increasing need to certify the validity and effectiveness of statistical inference in high-dimensional models, over recent years, there has been a renewed impetus to derive sharp Berry–Esseen bounds for Gaussian and bootstrap approximations for hyper-rectangles with explicit dependence on the dimension. This flurry of efforts has led to novel and optimal high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds. See Section
1.1
below for a brief summary of the literature and
Chernozhukov
et al. 2023
for a recent, more extensive review. These bounds can be described as follows in their most simplified and unified form. Let

\mathcal{R}_{p}=\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{p}[a_{k},b_{k}]\;\colon-\infty\leq a_{k}<b_{k}\leq\infty,k=1,\ldots,p\right\}

be the class of all
p
-dimensional hyper-rectangles, with possibly infinite sides. If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent, centered
p
-dimensional random vectors with non-degenerate (i.e. positive definite) covariances and eigenvalues bounded away from zero and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are independent, centered Gaussian random vectors with matching second moments, then

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}_{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\in A\right)\right|\leq C\frac{\mathrm{polylog}(p,n)}{\sqrt{n}}B_{q},

(1)

where
C
is a positive universal constant and
\mathrm{polylog}(p,n)
indicates polylogarithmic terms in
n
and
p
.
Above,
B_{q}
is a uniform bound on the
q
-th moment of the
X_{i}
’s, for
q\geq 3
, e.g.,

\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X_{i}\|^{q}_{\infty}\right]\leq B_{q},

where for a vector
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\|x\|_{\infty}=\max_{k=1,\ldots,p}|x_{k}|
. Assuming at least 4-moments (i.e.
q\geq 4
), the term
\mathrm{polylog}(p)
can be taken to be
\log^{3/2}(p)
, which is in fact optimal when the
X_{i}
’s are almost surely bounded
(
Chernozhukov
et al. 2023
)
. Possibly with a worse poly-logarithmic dependence on
p
, the bound also holds assuming only third-moment conditions
(
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
)
; in analogy with the univariate Berry–Esseen bound, this is the weakest possible result. Furthermore, the minimal eigenvalue condition can be relaxed to allow for vanishing eigenvalues and, under additional restrictions, the assumption of degenerate covariances can be lifted altogether
(
Fang et al. 2023a
)
.

The sharp high-dimensional Gaussian approximation bound (
1
) is predicated on the assumption of independent summands. In contrast, for normalized sums of
dependent
random vectors, fewer results are available and relatively little is known about the optimal dependence on the sample size and the dimension. See Section
1.1
below for a review of the current literature. In this paper, we close this gap by establishing a high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound of the form (
1
) for
m
-dependent
p
-dimensional random vectors. In detail, following the same settings outlined above,
Corollary

2.4
shows that

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}_{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\in A\right)\right|\leq C\frac{\mathrm{polylog}(p,n)}{\sqrt{n/m}}B_{q}.

(2)

The sequence
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
of random vectors satisfies the same conditions described above except for that of mutual independence; instead, they form an
m
-dependent sequence, i.e.

X_{i}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{j}\quad\text{if}\quad|i-j|>m,

(3)

for some integer
m
, which is allowed to depend on
n
, where the symbol
{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}
signifies stochastic independence. Like before, the
p
-dimensional Gaussian random vectors
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
have the same second moments as the
X_{i}
’s, in the sense that, for each
i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\mathrm{Var}[X_{i}]=\mathrm{Var}[Y_{i}]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}^{\top}]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}Y_{j}^{\top}].

Thus, the
Y_{i}
’s are also
m
-dependent.
A few remarks are in order.

•

The bound in
Equation

2
displays the optimal sample dependence rate
\sqrt{m/n}
, originally established in the univariate case
(
Shergin 1980
;
Chen and Shao 2004
)
. In comparison, the previously known best sample rate in high-dimensional cases was
m^{2/3}/n^{1/6}
, assuming sub-exponential random vectors
(
Chang, Chen and Wu 2021
, Theorem 2 and Corollary 1)
.

•

In the most general form of our result, we only require an average third moment condition, namely
B_{3}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{i}\|^{3}_{\infty}]<\infty
. As shown in
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
, this assumption cannot be dispensed of. Better dependence on the dimension is demonstrated by allowing higher order moments; see
Theorem

2.2
.

•

We further require the
Y_{i}
’s to satisfy minimal conditional variance restrictions, as discussed in Section
2
. To the best of our knowledge, these conditions are novel.

•

Our results also apply to sums of independent random vectors as a special case, delivering sharp or nearly sharp rates depending on the settings. In particular, we recover and, in fact, improve the bounds that a subset of the authors obtained in the preprint
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
. We further elaborate on this point in Section
2.3.1
.

•

We derive our bounds assuming a novel, general class of symmetric stochastic dependence, which we refer to as
m
-ring dependence, of which
m
-dependence is a special case; see Section
2.1

Regarding technical contributions, we deploy novel proof techniques generalizing the inductive arguments used in
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
that appear to be well suited to handle
m
-dependence settings and may be of independent interest.

Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
used a Lindeberg interpolation scheme coupled with induction (over
n
) to establish a high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for independent random variables with rates of order (ignoring terms depending on the minimal eigenvalues of the covariances)
(\log^{3}n\log^{8}(ep)/n)^{1/2}
. Though this approach is not new
(
Senatov 2011
;
Lopes 2020
, see, e.g.)
, the authors managed to refine it to obtain an optimal dependence on the sample size under minimal assumptions: non-degenerate covariances and finite third moments.
A key step in the proof of
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
is the use of the anti-concentration inequality for the Gaussian distribution
(
Nazarov 2003
)
. The importance of anti-concentration inequalities in obtaining Berry–Esseen bounds is well recognized in the literature; see, e.g.,
Bentkus et al. 2000
for an elucidation of how the Berry–Esseen bound follows from combining an induction over the number of samples along with anti-concentration inequalities. However, implicit in the arguments of
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
was the formulation of a backward relationship: anti-concentration bounds can, in turn, be derived from Berry–Esseen bounds. We further leverage this duality and deploy a novel induction argument on both the Berry–Esseen bound and the anti-concentration inequalities to obtain sharper bounds than those originally established by
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
for normalized sums of independent random vectors while, at the same time, allowing for
m
-dependence. See
Section

4
for a more detailed description of the dual induction.

The paper is organized as follows. In the next section, we summarize related works. We describe the settings and notation and present our main results in
Section

2
, which showcase the optimal scaling of
1/\sqrt{n}
under non-degenerate covariance and finite third moments. To establish these results, we used a combination of the Lindeberg swapping technique and a novel dual induction argument, overcoming the challenges posed by the
m
-dependence structure; we outline the proof techniques in
Section

4
. We then compare our results to existing works under independence and
m
-dependence in
Section

2.3
. Finally, in
Section

3
, we conclude the paper by summarizing the main findings and outlining avenues for future research.

1.1
Related work

In this section we summarize recent results about high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for hyper-rectangles, for independent and dependent summands. We refer the reader to
Chernozhukov
et al. 2023
for a thorough review article.

As already mentioned,
Bentkus 2003
derived a general high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for a wide variety of classes of Euclidean sets under only third moment conditions, assuming independent and isotropic summands. When applied to the important class convex sets, the resulting bound is non trivial only when
p=o(n^{2/7})
. See
Bentkus 2005
;
Raic̆ 2019
;
Fang and Koike 2021
;
Zhilova 2022
for extensions and sharpening of this original, fundamental results. In their pathbreaking contribution,
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2013
obtained a Berry–Esseen bound of order
(\log(np))^{7/8}n^{-1/8}
for the class of
p
-dimensional hyper-rectangles under appropriate conditions. (It is worth noting that, though the authors develop novel non-trivial and valuable techniques, in hindsight the same bound could have also been established using more direct and standard methods; see
Norvaiša and
Paulauskas 1991
.)
Subsequently, several authors generalized the settings of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2013
and improved on the original rate both in terms of dependence on the dimension
p
and of the exponent for the sample size
n
: see, in particular,
Deng and Zhang 2017
;
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017a
;
Kuchibhotla, Mukherjee and
Banerjee 2021
;
Koike 2019a
;
Koike 2019b
. For some time, it was a conjecture that the best dependence on the sample size, demonstrated by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017a
, should be of order
n^{-1/6}
, matching the optimal sample size dependence in general Banach spaces found by
Bentkus 1987
. This conjecture was later on disproved by multiple authors, who managed to improve the sample complexity beyond
n^{-1/6}
. In particular,
Chernozhukov
et al. 2022
managed to achieve a better dependence on the sample size of order
n^{-1/4}
for sub-Gaussian vectors while only requiring
\log(ep)=o(n^{1/5})
. Next,
Fang and Koike 2020a
,
Das and Lahiri 2020
, and
Lopes 2020
succeeded in going beyond a
n^{-1/4}
dependence. The most noticeable difference between these latest contributions compared to the earlier papers is that the random vectors are assumed to be non-degenerate, i.e., the minimum eigenvalue of the covariance matrix of the normalized sum is bounded away from zero. In detail,
Fang and Koike 2020a
used Stein’s method to sharpen the dependence on the sample size to
n^{-1/3}
, while at the same time weakening the requirement on the dimension to
\log(ep)=o(n^{1/4})
.
Fang and Koike 2020a
obtained an optimal
n^{-1/2}
dependence on the sample size along with a dimension requirement of
\log(ep)=o(n^{1/3})
, for sums of random vectors with log-concave distributions.

Das and Lahiri 2020
also established a
n^{-1/2}
dependence when the random vectors have independent and sub-Gaussian coordinates (among other assumptions); they also investigated the optimal dependence on the dimension.
Lopes 2020
finally succeeded in showing the optimal
n^{-1/2}
rate in general settings, assuming only sub-Gaussian vectors. A subset of the authors of this paper weakened considerably Lopes’ assumptions and demonstrated a bound of order
(\log^{3}n\log^{8}(ep)/n)^{1/2}
under only third-moment conditions; see
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
. Though the moment requirements are shown to be the weakest possible, the rate suffers from a suboptimal dependence on the dimension. Finally,
Chernozhukov
et al. 2023
obtained the sharper rate
(\log^{3}(ep)/n)^{1/2}
, which is, in fact, optimal for distributions with bounded support under fourth-moment conditions.
Fang et al. 2023b
further shows that the same rate was shown to hold also in the degenerate case under appropriate restrictions.

Overall, the aforementioned contributions offer a near-complete picture of optimal Gaussian and bootstrap approximations for independent normalized sums of random vectors with respect to the class of hyper-rectangles, with rates exhibiting optimal sample and dimension complexity. On the other hand, the literature on high-dimensional central limit theorems – over hyper-rectangles or other classes of sets in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
– for sums of
dependent random vectors
is still scarce, with relatively few results available and very limited understanding of the optimality. Furthermore, some of the best-known results at this time have been established by adapting arguments and techniques from
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2013
;
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017a
, which yield sub-optimal sample complexity. In turn, this is likely affecting the sharpness of current rates derived under dependence settings. Below we provide a (necessarily) short summary of the existing contributions, which are all very recent.
Zhang and Wu 2017
derive asymptotic Gaussian approximations over hyper-rectangles for high-dimensional stationary time series under a polynomial decay of the physical dependence measure, including
m
-dependent sequences, obtaining rates with sample size dependence of order
n^{-1/8}
.
Zhang and Cheng 2018
consider instead the broader class of weakly dependent, possibly non-stationary sequences as well as
m
-dependent data assuming a geometric moment condition under physical/functional dependence.
Chang, Chen and Wu 2021
provide high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for various classes of sets, including hyper-rectangles and the larger class of convex sets, under
\alpha
mixing, physical dependence and
m
-dependence. conditions.
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2019
investigated the validity of the block multiplier bootstrap under
\beta
-mixing.
Chiang, Kato and
Sasaki 2021
derive high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for sums of high-dimensional exchangeable array assuming sub-exponential conditions or
4+\delta
moments, for
\delta>0
.

Kojevnikov and
Song 2022
obtain a finite sample central limit approximation over hyper-rectangles of order
(\log^{5}(ep)/n)^{1/4}
for martingale difference sequences. The rate of
n^{-1/4}
is likely unimprovable, as it is known to be optimal in the
p=1

(
Bolthausen 1982
)
.
Kurisu, Kato and
Shao 2023
study Gaussian approximations over high-dimensional random fields under
\beta
-mixing spatial dependence structure, obtaining a rate of
n^{-1/12}

2
High-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for
m
-ring dependent random samples with nondegenerate covariance matrices

In this section, we present novel high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for
m
-dependent random vectors with non-degenerate covariance matrices. It is sufficient to consider the simplest case of
m=1
; the general case will follow from a standard argument: see Corollary
2.4
.
Before stating our main results, we introduce some necessary notation and concepts.

2.1
Setting and Assumptions

Throughout,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
denotes a time series of centered random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with at least
q\geq 3
moments, i.e.
\max_{i}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{i}\|^{q}_{\infty}]<\infty
. We assume the
X_{i}
’s satisfy a novel notion of stochastic dependence that we refer to as
m
-ring dependence.
In detail,
m
-ring dependence is in effect if
X_{i}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{j}
for any
i
and
j
satisfying
\min\{\lvert i-j\rvert,n-\lvert i-j\rvert\}>m
. It is easy to see that
m
-ring dependence is a generalization of
m
-dependence, as defined in
3
. Indeed, if
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are
m
-dependent, then

\min\{\lvert i-j\rvert,n-\lvert i-j\rvert\}>m\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lvert i-j\rvert>m\quad\Rightarrow\quad X_{i}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{j}.

Thus, if
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
m
dependent, they are also
m
-ring dependent. The notion of
m
-ring dependence is technically advantageous because of its higher degree of symmetry compared to
m
-dependence.

We will couple the
m
-ring dependent sequence
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
with a sequence of centered
p
-dimensional Gaussian random vectors
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
with matching covariances, in the sense that

\operatorname{Var}[(Y_{1}^{\top},\dots,Y_{n}^{\top})^{\top}]=\operatorname{Var}[(X_{1}^{\top},\dots,X_{n}^{\top})^{\top}].

Thus, it the
X_{i}
’s are
m
-ring dependent, so are the
Y_{i}
’s.

For any non-empty subset
I\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, define

\mathscr{X}_{I}:=\{X_{i}:i\in I\},~~\mathscr{Y}_{I}:=\{Y_{i}:i\in I\}.

and

X_{I}:=\sum_{i\in I}X_{i},~~Y_{I}:=\sum_{i\in I}Y_{i}.

(4)

To streamline our discussion, we introduce a special notation for integral intervals. For any pair of integers
i
and
j
that satisfy
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we will denote the ordered index set
\{i,\dots,j\}
as
[i,j]
. Accordingly,
[1,n]
will refers to the complete time course. To align with the conventions of real intervals, we will employ parentheses to represent open-ended intervals; e.g.,
(1,n]
denotes the set
\{2,\dots,n\}
. With this notation in place, our goal is to derive finite sample bounds for the quantity

\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}_{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}X_{[1,n]}\in A\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}Y_{[1,n]}\in A\right)\right|

(5)

To that effect, in order to ensure non-trivial results, we impose appropriate conditions on the conditional variances of the
Y_{i}
’s which, to the best of our knowledge, are novel. In detail, for any
I\subset[1,n]
, let
{\sigma}_{\min,I}
and
\underline{\sigma}_{I}
be

\displaystyle{\sigma}^{2}_{\min,I}:=

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[p]}\operatorname{Var}[Y^{(k)}_{I}],\quad\mbox{and}\quad\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{I}:=\lambda_{\min}(\operatorname{Var}[Y_{I}|\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}}]),

where the superscript
Y_{I}^{(k)}
notates each
k
-th element of
p
-dimensional random vector
Y_{I}
(as defined in
Eq.

4
), and
I^{\mathsf{C}}
is the index set complement. i.e.,
I^{\mathsf{C}}\equiv[1,n]\setminus I
.
We note that the conditional variance is given by the Schur complement of the marginal covariance matrix:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}[Y_{I}|\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}}]=\operatorname{Var}[Y_{I}]-\operatorname{Cov}[Y_{I},\mathrm{vec}(\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}})]\operatorname{Var}[\mathrm{vec}(\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}})]^{-1}\operatorname{Cov}[\mathrm{vec}(\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}}),Y_{I}],

where
\mathrm{vec}(\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}})
indicates the vectorized representation by concatenation. i.e.,
\mathrm{vec}(\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}})\equiv(Y_{i}^{\top}:i\in I^{\mathsf{C}})^{\top}
. In case
m=0
, i.e. when
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are independent, then

\operatorname{Var}[Y_{I}|\mathscr{Y}_{I^{\mathsf{C}}}]=\operatorname{Var}[Y_{I}].

We assume that there exist constants
\sigma_{\min},\underline{\sigma}>0
such that for all
I=[i,j]
or
[j,n]\cup[1,i]
with some
1\leq i<j\leq n
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{\min,I}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}_{\min}\cdot\lvert I\rvert\cdot\min\{m+1,\lvert I\rvert\},

(MIN-VAR)

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{I}

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}\cdot\max\{\lvert I\rvert-2m,0\}\cdot\min\{m+1,\max\{\lvert I\rvert-2m,0\}\},

(MIN-EV)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}

\displaystyle\leq\underline{\sigma}\sqrt{\log(4ep)/2},

(VAR-EV)

where
\lvert I\rvert
is the number of elements in
I
.
The assumption of strongly non-degenerate covariance (i.e.,
\lambda_{\min}(\operatorname{Var}[X_{i}])
is bounded away from zero) has been commonly used in high-dimensional CLTs under independence
(
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
;
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
;
Fang and Koike 2021
;
Lopes 2022
)
. Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
) and (
MIN-EV
) impose analogous restrictions to
m
-(ring) dependent sequences; they are inspired by Assumption (3) in
Shergin 1980
in the univariate case. It is worth highlighting that Assumption (
MIN-EV
) accommodates the possibility of complete dependence between
X_{I}
and adjacent elements, for
\lvert I\rvert\leq 2m
. For example, consider a scenario where
X_{2j-1}=X_{2j}
for
j=1,\dots,\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil
, while
X_{1},X_{3},\dots
are independent. In this case,
\sigma^{2}_{[2,3]}=\operatorname{Var}[Y_{[2,3]}|Y_{1},Y_{4},Y_{5},\dots,Y_{n}]=0
because
Y_{2}
and
Y_{3}
exhibit total dependence with
Y_{1}
and
Y_{4}
, respectively. However,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
still convergences weakly to
\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
. Our Berry–Esseen bounds apply to this scenario.
Finally, assumption (
VAR-EV
) is made out of technical convenience and is not necessary. It may be removed, leading to a more complicated upper bound expression; see
Remark

2.3
for details.

We will also consider two different types of moment bounds: a marginal one on the individual coordinates of the
X_{i}
’s and of the
Y_{i}
’s, and a stronger one involving their
L_{\infty}
norm. Specifically, for
q\geq 1
and
i=1\ldots,n
, let

L_{q,i}:=\max_{k=1,\ldots,p}\mathbb{E}[\lvert X_{i}^{(k)}\rvert^{q}]+\max_{k=1,\ldots,p}\mathbb{E}[\lvert Y_{i}^{(k)}\rvert^{q}],\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nu_{q,i}:=\mathbb{E}[\lVert X_{i}\rVert_{\infty}^{q}]+\mathbb{E}[\lVert Y_{i}\rVert_{\infty}^{q}].

(6)

Clearly,
L_{q,i}\leq\nu_{q,i}
for all
q
and
i
.
For any subset
I\subset[1,n]
, we denote the average marginal and joint moments over the indices in
I
with

\overline{L}_{q,I}=\frac{1}{\lvert I\rvert}\sum_{j\in I}L_{q,j}\enskip\text{and}\enskip\overline{\nu}_{q,I}=\frac{1}{\lvert I\rvert}\sum_{j\in I}\nu_{q,j}.

(7)

and write
\overline{L}_{q}=\overline{L}_{q,[1,n]}
and
\overline{\nu}_{q}=\overline{\nu}_{q,[1,n]}
for the global average moments.
We note that, due to Jensen’s inequality,
\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}\nu_{2,i}\leq\nu_{q,i}^{2/q}
and
\overline{\nu}_{2}\leq\overline{\nu}_{q}^{2/q}
(since
q>2
).

2.2
Main Results

With the notations and assumptions in place, we are now ready to present our first bound on the quantity
\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)
(see
5
), assuming only finite third moments.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose that Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
), (
MIN-EV
) and (
VAR-EV
) hold. Then, for
m=1
and
q\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\log(en)}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(pn)}{n}}\left[\frac{\overline{L}_{3}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{2}(ep)+\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{1/(q-2)}\log^{1\vee 2/(q-2)}(ep)\right],

for some universal constant
C>0
.

The main steps of the proof of a simplified and weaker version of the above statement that depends on the
maximal
, as opposed to average, third moments are described in detail in
Section

4
. The complete proof calls for a more complicated and cumbersome Lindeberg swapping argument that will be explained in
Section

4.6
. For the comprehensive proof, please refer to
Section

A.3
.

It is worth commenting on the dimension complexity (i.e. the overall dependence on
p
) of the bound in
Theorem

2.1
. The distance
\mu(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]})
, will vanish as
n
,
p
and all the quantities involved vary provided that

\max\left\{\frac{\overline{L}_{3}^{2}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{4}}\log^{5}p,\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}^{2/(q-2)}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{4/(q-2)}}(\log p)^{4/(q-2)}\right\}=o(n).

(8)

To illustrate, if
\sigma_{\min}
and
\underline{\sigma}
are of constant order, the dimension complexity will depend on how the terms
\overline{L}_{3}^{2}\log^{5}(p)
and
\overline{\nu}_{q}^{2/(q-2)}(\log p)^{4/(q-2)}
scale with
n
.
For instance, if the
X_{i}
’s are sub-Gaussian, the dimension complexity is dominated by the first term in the left hand side of (
8
), and the requirement becomes
\log^{5}p=o(n)
.
Under the same settings and assuming independent summands (a special case of the above theorem; see
Section

2.3.1
below), the best-known dimension complexity in the literature is
\log^{4}p=o(n)
; see
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
, Corollary 2.1
.)
This sharper dimension complexity rate was obtained assuming at least four moments, i.e.
q\geq 4
. Under this stronger assumption we are in fact able to match this rate. This is not surprising, existence of higher moments has been shown to improve the dimension complexity: for example,
Fang and Koike 2020b
sharpened the the high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for convex sets established by
Bentkus 2005
assuming finite fourth moment conditions.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
), (
MIN-EV
) and (
VAR-EV
) hold. Then,
if
m=1
and
q\geq 4
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\log\left(en\right)}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(pn)}{n}}\left[\frac{\overline{L}_{3}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{3/2}(ep)+\frac{\overline{L}_{4}^{1/2}}{\underline{\sigma}}\log(ep)+\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{1/(q-2)}\log(ep)\right],

for some constant
C>0
.

The proof is similar to that of
Theorem

2.1
, as outlined in
Section

4
, with an additional step that takes advantage of the extra moment; for details see below
Section

4.4
. The complete proof can be found in
Section

A.4
.

In terms of rates,
Theorem

2.2
will require the asymptotic scaling

\max\left\{\frac{\overline{L}_{3}^{2}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{4}}\log^{4}p,\frac{\overline{L}_{4}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{3}p,\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}^{2/(q-2)}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{4/(q-2)}}\log^{3}p\right\}=o(n).

When the
X_{i}
’s are sub-Gaussian and
\sigma_{\min}
and
\underline{\sigma}
are of constant order, the above scaling reduces to
\log^{4}p=o(n)
, which matches that of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
. In
Section

2.3.1
, we will provide more general and in-depth comparisons of our rates with those of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
.

Remark 2.3
.

Assumption (
VAR-EV
) is not commonly found in the literature and may be considered restrictive in many practical applications. In fact, the Berry–Esseen bounds in
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
still hold even without this assumption. The only difference in the bounds is that the factor
{C}/{\sigma_{\min}}
in both
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
is replaced by
C/\min\{\sigma_{\min},\underline{\sigma}\sqrt{\log(ep)}\}
; see
Section

4.5
for an explanation of the changes in the proof.

Finally, Berry–Esseen bounds for general
m>1
can be obtained as corollaries of
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
using a standard argument; see, e.g.,
Shergin 1980
and
Chen and Shao 2004
.
In detail, let
n^{\prime}=\lfloor{n}/{m}\rfloor
,

X^{\prime}_{i}=X_{((i-1)m,im]}
for
i\in[1,n^{\prime})
and
X^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}=X_{((n^{\prime}-1)m,n]}
. We define
Y^{\prime}_{i}
similarly for
i\in[1,n^{\prime}]
. Then,
X^{\prime}_{1},\dots,X^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
are
1
-dependent random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Thus, by applying
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
to
\mu(X^{\prime}_{[1,n^{\prime}]},Y^{\prime}_{[1,n^{\prime}]})=\mu(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]})
, we immediately obtain the following results.

Corollary 2.4
.

Suppose that Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
), (
MIN-EV
), and (
VAR-EV
) hold and that
m>1
, and let
n_{\mathrm{eff}}\equiv n/m
.
If
q\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\log\left(en_{\mathrm{eff}}\right)}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(pn_{\mathrm{eff}})}{n_{\mathrm{eff}}}}\left[\frac{\overline{L}_{3}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{2}(ep)+\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{2}(ep)\right)^{1/(q-2)}\right],

for some universal constant
C>0
. If
q\geq 4
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(pn_{\mathrm{eff}})}{n_{\mathrm{eff}}}}\log\left(en_{\mathrm{eff}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[\frac{\overline{L}_{3}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{3/2}(ep)+\frac{\overline{L}_{4}^{1/2}}{\underline{\sigma}}\log(ep)+\left(\frac{\overline{\nu}_{q}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{1/(q-2)}\log(ep)\right],

for some universal constant
C>0
.

It is important to note that, ignoring logarithmic quantities, the sample complexity in the previous bounds is of order
\sqrt{m/n}
,
which matches the optimal Berry–Esseen rates for univariate
m
-dependent time series established in
Shergin 1980
; we remark on this below in
Section

2.3
.

2.3
Comparisons with the existing literature

In this section, we compare our main results with existing results in the high-dimensional CLT literature. In
Section

2.3.1
, we explore the implication of our bounds under independence and compare it with the nearly optimal result presented by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
. Though, in general, the bounds are not comparable, they imply the same dimension complexity for the important case of sub-Weibull
X_{i}
’s (including sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential cases). Importantly, beyond independent summands, our work delivers a significant improvement over existing results about Berry–Esseen bound under
m
-dependence; see
Section

2.3.2
.

2.3.1
Under independence

Let’s consider the case where the random variables
X_{i}
are independent. Since independence holds for all pairs of
X_{i}
, regardless of the difference between their indices, we can say that
X_{i}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{j}
when
\lvert i-j\rvert
is greater than or equal to 1 or, equivalently, when
i\neq j
. Thus, according to the definition of
1
-dependence, independence is a special case of
1
-dependence. As a result,
Theorem

2.1
and
Theorem

2.2
readily hold for sums of independent random vectors. Below, we compare our bounds with the those established by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
and
Fang et al. 2023b
, which deliver the sharpest rates to date. We first note that, under independence, assumptions (
MIN-EV
) and (
MIN-VAR
) reduce to

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[p]}\operatorname{Var}[Y^{(k)}_{[i,j]}]

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}_{\min}\cdot\lvert[i,j]\rvert,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\operatorname{Var}[Y_{[i,j]}])

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\sigma}^{2}\cdot\lvert[i,j]\rvert,~\forall i,j.

These assumptions require the covariance matrix of
X_{[i,j]}
to be strongly non-degenerate for all pairs
(i,j)
, a condition that is commonly imposed in order to derive high-dimensional CLTs.
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
do require non-degenerate covariance matrices by assuming that the covariance matrix of the scaled average is well-approximated by a positive-definite matrix. The resulting Berry–Esseen bound however depends on the quality of this approximation.
Importantly, a minimal eigenvalue condition is required on
the average
of the covariance matrices, while we take the minimal eigenvalue of all covariances; thus their result is more general in this aspect.
Recently,
Fang et al. 2023b
pursued a more direct approach and succeeded in obtaining a Berry–Esseen bound with the optimal
1/\sqrt{n}
-rate allowing for degenerate covariance matrices, provided that they satisfy additional assumptions.
It would be interesting to explore an extension of their work in our setting.
In terms of moment assumption, our result is more general than those of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
and
Fang et al. 2023b
, which are predicated on finite fourth moments. In contrast,
Theorem

2.1
only assumes finite average third moments, thus accommodating a broader class of models.
For example, this scenario can occur in high-dimensional linear regression problems where
X_{i}=\xi_{i}W_{i}
for heavy-tailed univariate errors
\xi_{i}
and light-tailed covariates
W_{i}
. If
\xi_{i}
’s have a finite third (conditional on
W_{i}
) moment but an infinite fourth moment, then
\mathbb{E}\|X_{i}\|_{\infty}^{4}
can be infinite; see
Chernozhukov
et al. 2023
for an application to penalty parameter selection in lasso using bootstrap.

We now shift our focus to the overall convergence rate implied by our Berry–Esseen bounds, taking into account the dimension. Our theorems yields rates of order
1/\sqrt{n}
with respect to
n
up to logarithmic factors when
p
remains fixed. Therefore, a more meaningful comparison lies in the dimension complexities imposed by the theorems. As previously discussed, these dimension complexities are dependent on the tail behavior of the
X_{i}
’s. To exemplify, assume that the
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. and that
\operatorname{Var}[X_{j}^{(k)}]=1
for all
k=1,\dots,n
; consider the following scenarios:

1.

\lvert X_{j}^{(k)}\rvert\leq B
for all
j=1,\dots,n
and
k=1,\dots,p
almost surely;

2.

\lVert X_{j}^{(k)}\rVert_{\psi_{\alpha}}\leq B
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\lvert X_{i}^{(k)}\rvert^{4}]\leq B^{2}

for all
j=1,\dots,n
and
k=1,\dots,p
, where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\psi_{\alpha}}
is the Orlicz norm with respect to
\psi_{\alpha}(x)\equiv\exp(x^{\alpha})
for
\alpha\leq 2
.

These cases correspond conditions (E1) and (E2) respectively
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
; see Corollary 2.1 therein.
Comparing the Berry–Esseen bounds implied by
Theorem

2.2
with those from Corollary 2.1
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
, we have, in the first scenario,

\mu(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]})\leq\begin{cases}\frac{CB}{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{3/2}(ep)\log(en),&\mbox{ from Corollary 2.1, \cite[citealias]{\@@bibref{Phrase1}{chernozhukov2020nearly}{\@@citephrase{CCK20}}{}}},\\
\frac{CB}{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log^{3/2}(ep)\sqrt{\log(pn)}\log\left(en\right),&\mbox{ from our \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:1_dep_berry_esseen_4P}.}\end{cases}

The dimension complexity obtained by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
is
\log^{3}p=o(n)
, which is shown to be optimal; see Remark 2.1 in
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
. For our result, the bound is derived using the fact that
L_{3,j}\leq BL_{2,j}=B
,
L_{4,j}\leq B^{2}L_{2,j}=B^{2}
, and
\nu_{q,j}\leq B^{q}
for all
q\geq 4
. The resulting dimension complexity of
\log^{4}p=o(n)
. Therefore, our Berry–Esseen bound is suboptimal when the
X_{i}
’s are bounded random vectors.
Next, suppose that the individual elements of
X_{i}
are sub-Weibull
(\alpha)
for
\alpha\leq 2
. The cases
\alpha=1
and
\alpha=2
correspond to sub-Gaussian and sub-Exponential coordinates, respectively. In this scenario,

\displaystyle\mu(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]})

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{CB}{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\left(\log(en)\log^{3/2}(ep)+\log^{(3/2+1/\alpha)}(ep)\right),&\mbox{ from Corollary 2.1, \cite[citealias]{\@@bibref{Phrase1}{chernozhukov2020nearly}{\@@citephrase{CCK20}}{}}},\\
\frac{CB}{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\sqrt{\log(pn)}\log\left(en\right)\left(\log^{3/2}(ep)+\log^{(3/2+1/\alpha)}(ep)\right),&\mbox{ from our \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:1_dep_berry_esseen_4P}.}\end{cases}

Corollary 2.1 of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
only consider sub-Gaussian
X_{i}
’s, but the proof readily extends to the other cases with
\alpha<2
.
This leads to the dimension complexity of
\log^{3+2/\alpha}(ep)=o(n)
.
Our bounds stem from the fact that
L_{3,j}\leq\sqrt{L_{2,j}L_{4,j}}\leq B
,
L_{4,j}\leq B^{2}
, and
\nu_{q,j}\leq CB^{q}\sigma_{\min}^{q}\log^{q/\alpha}(ep)
for any
q\geq 4
(see Corollary 7.4,
Zhang and Chen 2020
). The resulting dimension complexity is the same as
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
.

2.3.2
Under
m
-dependence

In this section, we compare
Corollary

2.4
to existing CLT results for
m
-dependent random variables. Towards that goal, we find it helpful to recall the well-known Berry–Esseen bounds of
Shergin 1980
for
m
-dependent univariate (
p=1
) sequences. Theorem 2 of
Shergin 1980
yields that, for
q\geq 3
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(q,M,n_{0})\left[(m+1)^{q-1}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{q}]}{(\mathbb{E}[X_{[1,n]}^{2}])^{q/2}}\right]^{1/(q-2)},

under the assumption that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}[X_{i}]\leq M\cdot\operatorname{Var}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}]
for
n
larger than an appropriate
n_{0}>0
. We note that this assumption is similar to our Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
) and (
MIN-EV
). In particular, under (
MIN-VAR
),

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(q)\left[n^{1-q/2}(m+1)^{q-1}\frac{\overline{L}_{q}}{\sigma_{\min}^{q}}\right]^{1/(q-2)}

(9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(q)}{\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{eff}}}}\left[\frac{\overline{L}_{q}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\right]^{1/(q-2)},

where first inequality follows from the bound
\mathbb{E}[X_{[1,n]}^{2}]\geq n\sigma_{\min}^{2}
and the second from
\sigma_{\min}^{2}=\min\{m+1,n\}\underline{\sigma}^{2}
. Furthermore, the dependence on
m
cannot be improved, as shown in
Berk 1973
. Next, using the fact that, in the univariate case,
\mathbb{E}[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{q}]=L_{q,i}=\nu_{q,i}
for all
i
, we conclude that, aside from universal constant, the bounds we obtain in
Corollary

2.4
match Shergin’s bound (
9
), aside from logarithmic factors. What is more, the same remains true in the multivariate case, as long as
p
is fixed: the upper bound
Corollary

2.4
exhibits the same scaling in
n
and
m
as in the expression (
9
), up to logarithmic factors. This indicates that, in fixed dimensional settings, our bounds are essentially sharp.

Moving on to the multivariate case,
the literature on high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for
m
-dependent random vectors is relatively scarce.
Zhang and Cheng 2018
used a Berry–Esseen bound for
m
-dependent series to bound the weak physical processes. Though their results do not seem to provide explicit rates for
m
-dependent cases, those rates appear to be no faster
(m^{2}/\sqrt{n})^{1/4}
, which is slower than the rates we achieve; see their Theorem 2.1. More recently, by employing the large-small-block approach similar to the one used in
Romano and Wolf 2000
in the univariate cases,
Chang, Chen and Wu 2021
derived a bound of order
O({m^{2/3}\mathrm{polylog}(p,n)}{n^{-1/6}})
, under the strong assumption of sub-exponential random vectors.

To summarize, our bounds in
Corollary

2.4
yield a rate of order

O({\mathrm{polylog}(p,n)}{(n/m)^{-1/2}}),

which is essentially optimal and is achieved under minimal assumptions.

3
Discussion

We derived a
1/\sqrt{n}
scaling of Berry–Esseen bound for high-dimensional
m
-dependent random vectors over hyper-rectangles the weakest possible settings of finite third moments. For fixed
p
, the rate implied by our result is
\{\log(n/m)\}^{3/2}\sqrt{m/n}
, matching the optimal rate for
m
-dependent random variables obtained by
Shergin 1980
.

Our advancement in the Gaussian approximation rate of
m
-dependent samples could benefit the theoretical analyses under physical dependence frameworks.
Zhang and Cheng 2018
introduced the
m
-approximation technique to study the Gaussian approximation of weakly dependent time series under physical dependence. The technique extends the Berry–Esseen bounds for
m
-dependent samples to weaker temporal dependencies (see Theorem 2.1 and the end of Section 2.2 therein). Similarly,
Chang, Chen and Wu 2021
extended the
n^{-1/6}
rate under
m
-dependence to samples with physical dependence. The resulting rate in Theorem 3 was better than the best rate of
Zhang and Wu 2017
.

Another important future direction is extending our technique to samples with generalized graph dependency. Random vectors
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are said to have dependency structure defined by graph
G=([n],E)
if
X_{i}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{j}
if
(i,j)\in E
. Graph dependency generalizes
m
-dependence as a special case by taking
E=\{(i,j):\lvert i-j\rvert\leq m\}
. The only CLT result up to our best knowledge has been
Chen and Shao 2004
for
1
-dimensional samples with graph dependency. Extending their result to high-dimensional samples has a huge potential to advance statistical analyses on network data, which is another data type with increasing availability.

4
Proof techniques

The proofs of
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
are somewhat involved and contains multiple steps. To ease the reader in, below we sketch the proof for a simplified and weaker version of our bounds, assuming
3\leq q
and
1
-dependence, instead of
1
-ring dependence. In this particular case, we have
X_{1}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}X_{n}
. This results in a Berry–Esseen bound similar to the one presented
Theorem

2.1
, but with

L_{3,\max}\equiv\max_{i\in[1,n]}L_{3,i}\enskip\text{and}\enskip\nu_{q,\max}\equiv\max_{i\in[1,n]}\nu_{q,i}

in place of
\overline{L}_{3}
and
\overline{\nu}_{q}
, respectively.
In the proof, we use the inductive relationship between anti-concentration bounds and Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistics. Anti-concentration refers to the probability of a random variable to be contained in a small subset (typically an annulus).
An anti-concentration probability bound for the Gaussian distribution that is commonly used in the recent literature on high-dimensional central limit theorem is that established by

Nazarov 2003

(
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017b
, see also)
, who derived an upper bound for the probability that a Gaussian random vector is contained in
A_{r,\delta}\equiv\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:x\preceq r+\delta\mathbf{1}\}\setminus\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:x\preceq r-\delta\mathbf{1}\}
for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
.

Lemma 4.1
(Gaussian anti-concentration inequality;
Nazarov 2003
;
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2017b
).

For a random vector
Y\sim N(0,\Sigma)
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
,

\mathbb{P}[Y\in A_{r,\delta}]\leq C\delta\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{\min_{i=1,\dots,p}\Sigma_{ii}}}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

The relevant anti-concentration probability for our problem involves the conditional probability of all the partial sums of the data
X_{[1,i]}
,
i\leq n
, given
\{X_{j},j>i\}
. We denote the supremum of these probabilities over all possible choice of the parameter
r\in\mathbb{R}
by

\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\equiv\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{P}[{X}_{[1,i]}\in A_{r,\delta}|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}].

We also denote the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistics of interest by

\mu_{[1,i]}\equiv\mu({X}_{[1,i]},{Y}_{[1,i]}),

keeping track explicitly in our notation of the range of summation.
We start the proof by establishing the inductive relationship from
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
to
\mu_{[1,i]}
: for
i\leq n
and
\delta>\sigma_{\min}
,

\sqrt{i}\mu_{[1,i]}\leq C(\nu_{q},\sigma_{\min},\underline{\sigma})\delta\log(ep)+C(\nu_{q},\underline{\sigma})\frac{\log(en)(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta}\sup_{j:j<i}\sqrt{j}\kappa_{[1,j]}\left(\delta\right).

(10)

To derive this, we use the Lindeberg swapping technique, using a similar approach to the one in
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
. Our key contribution in this aspect lies in addressing the added complexity due to the dependence structure. For the details, refer to
Section

4.1
.

In light of
Eq.

10
, our objective is to provide an upper bound for the anti-concentration probabilities
\kappa_{[1,j]}(\delta)
. When the random variables
X_{j}
are independent,
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
represents the marginal anti-concentration probability since the condition on
\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}
in the definition of
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
can be omitted. Consequently, an upper bound for
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
can be obtained in a straightforward way (see
Lemma

4.1
), for any
i\leq n
:

\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\leq\mu_{[1,i]}+C\delta\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}n}}.

(11)

Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
implicitly employed a dual induction approach using
Eqs.

10
and
11
to establish the Berry–Esseen bound with the desired rate over
n
, i.e.,
1/\sqrt{n}
up to the logarithm. However,
Eq.

11
falls short when dealing with
1
-dependence, as
X_{[1,i]}
becomes dependent on
\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}
within the definition of
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
.
In the case of univariate dependent
X_{j}
,
Chen and Shao 2004
derived a non-inductive upper bound for the conditional anti-concentration probability using a telescoping method (refer to Proposition 3.2 therein). Extending this method to high-dimensional cases presents a non-trivial challenge. In our work, we adopt a similar intuition, but instead of aiming for a non-inductive bound, we establish an inductive relationship from
\mu_{[1,i]}
to
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
; specifically, for
i<n
and
\delta>\sigma_{\min}
, we show that

\sqrt{i}\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\leq C\left(\frac{\delta+\nu_{1}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\log(ep)}+\max_{j:j\leq i-2}\sqrt{j}\mu_{[1,j]}\right).

(12)

These key contributions are contained in
Lemma

4.5
in
Section

4.2
. Finally, we conduct a dual induction using
Eqs.

10
and
12
and conclude the proof.

The proofs of
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
share similar steps but incorporate additional techniques. The explanation of these techniques is provided in the last two subsections. In
Section

4.4
, we present the iterated Lindeberg swapping method, which helps improve the dimension complexity when a finite fourth moment condition is satisfied. Additionally, in
Section

4.6
, we introduce permutation arguments to enhance the Berry–Esseen bounds by replacing the maximal moments with the average moments. For the complete proofs, please refer to
Sections

A.3
and
A.4
.

4.1
Induction from
\kappa
to
\mu
for
3\leq q

Here we prove the inductive relationship for
\mu_{[1,n]}
, but the same proof applies to
\mu_{[1,i]}
with any
i
smaller than
n
.
The quantity we want to control concerns expectations of indicator functions, which are not smooth. For this reason, most proofs of CLTs apply a smoothing to replace indicator functions by smooth functions. We use the mixed smoothing proposed by
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
. Namely, for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\delta,\phi>0
, it holds that

\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(x)\equiv\mathbb{E}[f_{r,\phi}(x+\varepsilon Z)],

where

f_{r,\phi}(x)\equiv\begin{cases}1,&\text{if}~\max\{x^{(i)}-r^{(i)}:i\in[d]\}<0,\\
1-\phi\max\{x^{(i)}-r^{(i)}:i\in[d]\},&\text{if}~0\leq\max\{x^{(i)}-r^{(i)}:i\in[d]\}<1/\phi,\\
0,&\text{if}~1/\phi\leq\max\{x^{(i)}-r^{(i)}:i\in[d]\}.\end{cases}

This smoothing leads to a smoothing bias terms, which can be controlled using Lemma 1 of
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
and Lemma 2.1 of
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
. Here we summarize the two results into the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
X
is a
p
-dimensional random vector, and
Y\sim N(0,\Sigma)
is a
p
-dimensional Gaussian random vector. Then, for any
\delta>0
,

\mu(X,Y)\leq C\frac{\delta\log(ep)+\sqrt{\log(ep)}/\phi}{\sqrt{\min_{i=1,\dots,p}\Sigma_{ii}}}+C\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(X)]-\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(Y)]\right\rvert.

Because
\min_{i=1,\dots,p}\Sigma_{ii}\geq n\sigma_{\min}^{2}
,
Lemma

4.2
implies

\mu_{[1,n]}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\delta\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\phi\sigma_{\min}}+C\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(X_{[1,n]})]-\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(Y_{[1,n]})]\right\rvert.

Lindeberg swapping.

The standard Lindeberg swapping approach upper bounds

\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(X_{[1,n]})]-\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(Y_{[1,n]})]\right\rvert

by decomposing it into

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(X_{[1,n]})]-\mathbb{E}[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(Y_{[1,n]})]\right\rvert

(13)

\displaystyle=\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+X_{j})-\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+Y_{j})\right]\right\rvert,

where
W_{[i,j]}^{\mathsf{C}}\equiv X_{[1,i)}+Y_{(j,n]}
, and further bounding the each term by third-order remainder terms after Taylor expansions up to order
3
and the second moment matching between
X_{j}
and
Y_{j}
. In the subsequent discussion, we will use the notation
W
as a wildcard, representing either
X
or
Y
depending on the context.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+X_{j})]

(14)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]})]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}),X_{j}\right\rangle\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\nabla^{2}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}),X_{j}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{2}\left\langle\nabla^{3}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+tX_{j}),X_{j}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle~dt\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]})]+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathbb{E}[\nabla^{2}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]})],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 2}]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{3}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+tX_{j}),X_{j}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle\right]~dt,

so by
\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 2}]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{j}^{\otimes 2}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+X_{j})]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+Y_{j})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{3}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+tX_{j}),X_{j}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle\right]~dt

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{3}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+tX_{j}),Y_{j}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle\right]~dt.

However, in the case of
1
-dependence, the second equality of
Eq.

14
no longer holds due to the dependency between
W_{[j,j]}^{\mathsf{C}}
and
X_{j}
. To address this issue, we introduce Taylor expansions on
\nabla\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]})
and
\nabla^{2}\varphi_{r}^{\varepsilon}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]})
to break the dependency before proceeding with the second-order moment matching. This additional step involves meticulous calculations and lengthy specification of remainder terms. We provide the full details in
Section

C.2
.
As a result,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+X_{j})-\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(W^{\mathsf{C}}_{[j,j]}+Y_{j})\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(3,1)}-\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(3,1)}\right],

(15)

where
\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(3,1)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(3,1)}
are remainder terms of the Taylor expansions specified in
Section

C.2
.

Remainder lemma.
Then, we upper bound the remainder terms using the upper bounds for the differentials of
\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}
. In particular,
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
showed in Lemmas 6.1 and 6.2 that

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{\alpha}}\sup_{\lVert z\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2\delta}{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}}\left\lvert\nabla^{(i_{1},\dots,i_{\alpha})}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\delta}(w+z)\right\rvert\leq C\frac{\phi^{\gamma}(\log(ep))^{(\alpha-\gamma)/2}}{{\delta}^{\alpha-\gamma}}

for any
\gamma\in[0,1]
. For the event that
W_{[j,j]}^{\mathsf{C}}
is in the anulus
A_{r,\delta^{\prime}}
for some
\delta^{\prime}
we will specify shortly, we use the above inequality to bound the remainder term. Out of the event, the differential is sufficiently small. Hence, the upper bounds involves with the conditional anti-concentration probability
\mathbb{P}[W_{[j,j]}^{\mathsf{C}}\in A_{r,\delta^{\prime}}|X_{[1,j)}]
, resulting into the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that Assumption (
MIN-EV
) holds. For
W
representing either
X
or
Y
and
j\in[1,n]
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{W_{j}}^{(3,1)}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[L_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\min\{1,\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa^{o}_{j}\},

where
\delta_{n-j}^{2}=\delta^{2}+\underline{\sigma}^{2}\max\{n-j,0\}
,
\delta^{o}_{n-j}=12\delta_{n-j}\sqrt{\log(pn)}
and
\kappa^{o}_{j}=\frac{\delta_{n-j}\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{\max\{j,1\}}}
, as long as
\delta\geq\sigma_{\min}
and
\phi\delta\geq\frac{1}{\log(ep)}
.

Putting all the results back to
\mu_{[1,n]}
, we get

\displaystyle\mu_{[1,n]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\delta\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\phi\sigma_{\min}}+C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(3,1)}-\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(3,1)}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\delta\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\phi\sigma_{\min}}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[L_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\min\{1,\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa^{o}_{j}\}.

Partitioning the sum.

Let
J_{n}\equiv n(1-\frac{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\log^{2}(4ep)})
. We note that by Assumption (
VAR-EV
),
J_{n}\geq\frac{n}{2}
. The choice of
J_{n}
stems from comparing
1
and
\kappa_{[1,j)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa_{j}^{o}
. In detail, note that

\kappa_{[1,j)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa_{j}^{o}\geq 1

if
\kappa_{j}^{o}=\frac{\delta_{n-j}\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{\max\{j,1\}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\delta^{2}+\underline{\sigma}^{2}\max\{n-j,0\}}\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{\max\{j,1\}}}\geq 1
.

For
j<J_{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j<J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\min\{1,\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa^{o}_{j}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j<J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{5/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right],

because

\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor J_{n}\rfloor}\frac{1}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\leq\int_{n-\lfloor J_{n}\rfloor}^{n}\frac{1}{(\delta^{2}+t\underline{\sigma}^{2})^{3/2}}dt\leq-\frac{2}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\delta_{n}}+\frac{2}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\delta_{n-\lfloor J_{n}\rfloor}}\leq\frac{C}{\underline{\sigma}^{3}\sqrt{n-J_{n}}}\leq\frac{C\log(ep)}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n}}.

(16)

For
j\geq J_{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\min\{1,\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})+\kappa^{o}_{j}\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa^{o}_{j}.

The last term is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa^{o}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{5/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right)\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right].

because

\sum_{j=\lceil J_{n}\rceil}^{n}\frac{\kappa^{o}_{j}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}=\sum_{j=\lceil J_{n}\rceil}^{n}\frac{\log(ep)}{\delta_{n-j}^{2}\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{j}}\leq\frac{C\log(ep)}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{n}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right).

(17)

In sum, we obtain the following lemma about the relationship between
\mu_{[1,n]}\equiv\mu(X_{[1,n]},Y_{[1,n]})
and the conditional anti-concentration probability
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\equiv\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{P}[X_{[1,i]}\in A_{r,\delta}|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

If Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
), (
MIN-EV
) and (
VAR-EV
) hold, and
q\geq 3
, then
for any
\delta\geq\sigma_{\min}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{[1,n]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\delta\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\phi\sigma_{\min}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{5/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right)\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]

\displaystyle+C\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa_{[1,j-3)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o}),

for some absolute constant
C>0
.

4.2
Induction from
\mu
to
\kappa

Having obtained the induction from
\kappa
to
\mu
in the previous subsection, we now proceed to obtain an induction from
\mu
to
\kappa
. This is the step that was implicitly used in the proofs of high-dimensional CLTs for independent observations (e.g.,
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
). However, as mentioned in
Section

4
, the dependence between
X_{[1,i]}
and
\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}
in
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\equiv\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\mathbb{P}[{X}_{[1,i]}\in A_{r,\delta}|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]
makes the step non-trivial. We make a breakthrough using a similar approach described in
Section

4.1
, where we used the Taylor expansion to eliminate the dependency. However, once again, the conditional anti-concentration probability involves a conditional expectation of an indicator function, which lacks smoothness. So we first apply a smoothing technique to the indicator function and leverage the Taylor expansion on the smoothed indicator, subsequently bounding the resulting remainder terms.

Smoothing.

For the conditional anti-concentration probability, we use a standard smoothing, rather than the mixed smoothing we used in
Section

4.1
.
For
r\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\delta\in[0,\infty)
, let

\varphi_{r,\delta}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{I}\{x+\varepsilon Z\in A_{r,\delta}\}],

where
Z
is the
p
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector.
For some
h>0
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{r,\delta}^{\varepsilon}(x)-\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\}

\displaystyle=\int(\mathbb{I}\{x+\varepsilon z\in A_{r,\delta}\}-\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\})\phi(z)dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\lVert z\rVert_{\infty}\leq 10\sqrt{\log(ph)}}(\mathbb{I}\{x+\varepsilon z\in A_{r,\delta}\}-\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\})\phi(z)dz

\displaystyle+\int_{\lVert z\rVert_{\infty}>10\sqrt{\log(ph)}}(\mathbb{I}\{x+\varepsilon z\in A_{r,\delta}\}-\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\})\phi(z)dz

\displaystyle\geq-\mathbb{I}\{\lVert x-\partial A_{r,\delta}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 10\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(ph)}\}\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\}

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}[\lVert Z\rVert_{\infty}>10\sqrt{\log(ph)}],

where
\partial A_{r,\delta}
is the boundary of
A_{r,\delta}
,
Z
is the
p
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector and
\phi(z)
is the density function of
Z
. Hence,

\displaystyle\varphi_{r,\delta}^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\}-\mathbb{I}\{\lVert x-\partial A_{r,\delta}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 10\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(ph)}\}\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\}

(18)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{P}[\lVert Z\rVert_{\infty}>10\sqrt{\log(ph)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta-\varepsilon^{o}}\}-\frac{1}{h^{4}},

where
\varepsilon^{o}=10\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(ph)}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\varphi_{r,\delta}^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta}\}+\mathbb{I}\{\lVert x-\partial A_{r,\delta}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 10\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(ph)}\}\mathbb{I}\{x\notin A_{r,\delta}\}

(19)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}[\lVert Z\rVert_{\infty}>10\sqrt{\log(ph)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}\{x\in A_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}\}+\frac{1}{h^{4}}.

As a result, for any
h>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X_{[1,i]}\in A_{r,\delta}|X_{(i,n]}]

(20)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]})|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]+\frac{1}{h^{4}}.

Taylor expansion.

Applying the Taylor expansion to
\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]})|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]})|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]}-X_{\{2,i-1\}})|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{\{2,i-1\}},1}|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}\right]

where

\mathfrak{R}_{X_{\{2,i-1\}},1}=\int_{0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]}-tX_{\{2,i-1\}}),X_{\{2,i-1\}}\right\rangle dt

First, using
Eq.

19
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{r,\delta+\varepsilon^{o}}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,i]}-X_{\{2,i-1\}})|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{I}\{X_{[1,i]}-X_{\{2,i-1\}}\in A_{r,\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}\}+\frac{1}{h^{4}}|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}[X_{[3,i-2]}\in A_{r_{1},\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}|\mathscr{X}_{(i-1,n]\cup\{1\}}]|\mathscr{X}_{(i,n]}]+\frac{1}{h^{4}},

where
r_{1}=r-X_{1}-X_{i}
is a Borel measurable function with respect to
\mathscr{X}_{(i-1,n]\cup\{1\}}
. Because
X_{[3,i-2]}{\perp\!\!\!\!\perp}\mathscr{X}_{(i-1,n]\cup\{1\}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X_{[3,i-2]}\in A_{r_{1},\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}|\mathscr{X}_{(i-1,n]\cup\{1\}}]

(21)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}[Y_{[3,i-2]}\in A_{r_{1},\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}]+2\mu(X_{[3,i-2]},Y_{[3,i-2]})

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\delta+20\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(ph)}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{i_{0}-2}}+2\mu_{[3,i-2]},

almost surely due to the Gaussian anti-concentration inequality (
Lemma

4.1
).

Bounding the remainder.

Bounding the remainder term proceeds similarly with the proof of the remainder lemma (
Lemma

4.3
), resulting into an upper bound with an conditional anti-concentration probability bound (
\kappa_{(2,i-1)}(\epsilon^{o})
in the following lemma). We relegate the bounding details to
Section

B.4
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that Assumptions (
MIN-VAR
) and (
MIN-EV
) hold.
For any
i\in[3,n)
and
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\varepsilon}~(\nu_{1,2}+\nu_{1,i-1})~\kappa_{(2,i-1)}(\varepsilon^{o})+\mu_{(2,i-1)}\right)

\displaystyle+\min\left\{1,C\frac{\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{i-2}}\right\}+\frac{C}{\sigma_{\min}}(\nu_{1,2}+\nu_{1,i-1})\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{i-2}},

where
\varepsilon^{o}\equiv 20\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(p(i_{o}-2))}
, as long as
\varepsilon\geq\sigma_{\min}
.

This resembles the relationship within
\kappa
described in Eq. (3.16) of
Chen and Shao 2004
. In their work on univariate observations, however, the
\kappa
in the right hand side was
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\epsilon^{o})
instead of with the reduced index set
(2,i-1)
. Hence, they could plug-in
\delta=\epsilon^{o}
and upper bound
\kappa_{(2,i-1)}(\epsilon^{o})
for some suitable
\epsilon
, which we referred to ’a telescoping method’ in
Section

4
. This resulted into a non-inductive upper bound for
\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)
(see Eqs. (3.17) and (3.18) therein). In our setting, the reduced index set
(2,i-1)
makes the technique ineffective. Alternatively, we proceed to the dual induction levering on the reduced index set.

4.3
Dual Induction

Let our induction hypothesis on
n
be

\sqrt{n}\mu_{[1,n]}\leq\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n}L_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)},

(HYP-BE-1)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}_{1}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(en\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}_{2}\frac{\log(ep)\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2/(q-2)}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(en\right)

for universal constants
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
whose values do not change in this subsection.
If
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}\geq 1
, then (
HYP-BE-1
), requiring
\mu_{[1,n]}\leq 1
only, trivially holds for
n\leq 4
.

Now we consider the case of
n>4
. Suppose that the induction hypotheses hold for all intervals with lengths smaller than
n
. We first derive an anti-concentration inequality for any such intervals. Without loss of generality, we only consider the intervals
[1,i]
with
i<n
. We claim that

\sqrt{i}\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)\leq\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,i}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i}\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}\delta,

(HYP-AC-1)

where
\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i}=\mathfrak{C}_{1,\kappa}\widetilde{\mu}_{1,i}
,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,i}=\mathfrak{C}_{2,\kappa}\widetilde{\mu}_{2,i}
,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i}=\mathfrak{C}_{3,\kappa}\frac{\log(ep)\sqrt{\log(pi)}}{\sigma_{\min}}
and

\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}=\mathfrak{C}_{4,\kappa}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\sigma_{\min}}
for some universal constants
\mathfrak{C}_{1,\kappa}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{2,\kappa}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{3,\kappa}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{4,\kappa}
whose values do not change in this subsection. If
\mathfrak{C}_{1,\kappa},\mathfrak{C}_{2,\kappa},\mathfrak{C}_{3,\kappa},\mathfrak{C}_{4,\kappa}\geq 1
, then (
HYP-BE-1
), requiring
\kappa_{[1,n]}(\delta)\leq 1
for all
\delta>0
only, trivially holds for
i\leq 16
. For
i>16
, assume that
Eq.

HYP-AC-1
holds for all smaller
i
’s.
By
Lemma

4.5
,
for any
\varepsilon\geq\sigma_{\min}
and
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\varepsilon}\nu_{1,\max}\min\{1,\kappa_{(1,i)}(\varepsilon^{o})\}

\displaystyle+C\mu_{(1,i)}+C\frac{\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{i-2}}+C\frac{\nu_{1,\max}}{\sigma_{\min}}\frac{\log(ep)}{\sqrt{i-2}},

where
\varepsilon^{o}=20\varepsilon\sqrt{\log(p(i-2))}
and
C>0
is an absolute constant.
Due to (
HYP-AC-1
) and (
HYP-BE-1
) on interval
(1,i)\subsetneq[1,i]
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{i-2}}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\varepsilon}\nu_{1,\max}\left[\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i-2}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,i-2}{\nu}_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i-2}\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\sqrt{i-2}}\widetilde{\mu}_{1,i-2}{{L}_{3,\max}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{i-2}}\widetilde{\mu}_{2,i-2}{{\nu}_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}}

\displaystyle+C\frac{\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(ep)}{i-2}}+C\frac{\nu_{1,\max}}{\sigma_{\min}}\frac{\log(ep)}{\sqrt{i-2}}.

As a result, we obtain a recursive inequality on
\widetilde{\kappa}
’s that

\displaystyle\sqrt{i}\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)

(22)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\varepsilon}\nu_{1,\max}\left[\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i-2}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,i-2}{\nu}_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i-2}\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}\delta\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\left[\widetilde{\mu}_{1,i-2}{{L}_{3,\max}}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,i-2}{{\nu}_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}}+\frac{\delta+2\varepsilon^{o}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\log(ep)}+\frac{\nu_{1,\max}}{\sigma_{\min}}\log(ep)\right],

for some universal constant
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
, whose value does not change in this subsection.
Plugging in
\varepsilon=2\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(ep)}{\nu}_{2,\max}^{1/2}\geq 2\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(ep)}{\nu}_{1,\max}\geq\sigma_{\min}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{i}\kappa_{[1,i]}(\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left[\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i-2}{{L}_{3,\max}}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i-2}{{\nu}_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i-2}{\nu_{1,\max}}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}{\delta}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\left[\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mu}_{1,i-2}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,i-2}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}\end{aligned}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\frac{\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}{\nu}_{1,\max}+40\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\frac{\log(ep)\sqrt{\log(p(i-2))}}{\sigma_{\min}}{\nu}_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\sigma_{\min}}\delta

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,i}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,i}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,i}\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}\delta,

where
\mathfrak{C}_{1,\kappa}=\mathfrak{C}_{2,\kappa}=\mathfrak{C}_{4,\kappa}=\max\{2\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},1\}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{3,\kappa}=\max\{82\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},1\}
.

Now we prove (
HYP-BE-1
) on
n
. We first upper bound the last term in
Lemma

4.4
:

\displaystyle C\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o}).

Applying (
HYP-AC-1
) to
\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})
in
\mathfrak{T}_{2,1}
,

\displaystyle C\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}\sqrt{\max\{j-2,1\}}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\begin{aligned} &\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,j-2}{L}_{3,\max}&+\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,j-2}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,j-2}\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}+\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}\delta_{n-j}^{o}\\
\end{aligned}\right]

We recall that
\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,n-1}=\mathfrak{C}_{1,\kappa}\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n-1}
,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,n-1}=\mathfrak{C}_{2,\kappa}\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n-1}
,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{3,n-1}=\mathfrak{C}_{3,\kappa}\frac{\log(ep)\sqrt{\log(p(n-1))}}{\sigma_{\min}}
and

\widetilde{\kappa}_{4}=\mathfrak{C}_{4,\kappa}\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\sigma_{\min}}
.
Based on Eq. (15) in
Kuchibhotla and
Rinaldo 2020
, saying

\displaystyle\sum_{j=\lceil J_{n}\rceil}^{n}\frac{1}{\delta_{n-j}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right),

(23)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=\lceil J_{n}\rceil}^{n}\frac{1}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\delta\underline{\sigma}^{2}},

we obtain

\displaystyle C\sum_{j\geq J_{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\delta_{n-j}^{3}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\kappa_{[1,j-1)}(\delta_{n-j}^{o})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\begin{aligned} &(\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n-1}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n-1}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)})\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{\log(ep)\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\sigma_{\min}}\frac{\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}}{\delta}\\
&+\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)\log(pn)}}{\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right)\end{aligned}\right].

In sum, as long as
\delta\geq\nu_{2,\max}^{1/2}\sqrt{\log(ep)}\geq\sigma_{\min}
and
\phi>0
, we have the recursive inequality on
\widetilde{\mu}
’s that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mu_{[1,n]}

(24)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\delta}\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\left[\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\mu}_{1,n-1}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n-1}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}\end{aligned}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}\left[\frac{\delta\log(ep)}{\sigma_{\min}}+\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}{\phi\sigma_{\min}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}\frac{(\log(ep))^{5/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right)\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}\log\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{n}\underline{\sigma}}{\delta}\right)\left[{L}_{3,\max}+\phi^{q-3}\nu_{q,\max}\right]\frac{\sqrt{\log(ep)\log(pn)}}{\sigma_{\min}},

where
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}
is a universal constant whose value does not change in this subsection.
Taking
\delta=\frac{\max\{4\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime},1\}}{\sqrt{\log(ep)}}\left(\frac{{L}_{3,\max}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(\log(ep))^{2}+\left(\frac{\nu_{q,\max}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}(\log(ep))^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{q-2}}\right)\geq\overline{\nu}_{2}^{1/2}\sqrt{\log(ep)}
and
\phi=\frac{1}{\delta\sqrt{\log(ep)}}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mu_{[1,n]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\max_{j<n}\widetilde{\mu}_{1,j}{L}_{3,\max}+\frac{1}{2}\max_{j<n}\widetilde{\mu}_{2,j}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{C}^{(3)}\left({L}_{3,\max}\frac{(\log(ep))^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}}+\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)}\frac{(\log(ep))^{2/(q-2)}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2/(q-2)}}\right)\frac{\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(en\right)

for another universal constant
\mathfrak{C}^{(3)}
, whose value only depends on
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}
.
Taking
\mathfrak{C}_{1}=\mathfrak{C}_{2}=\max\{2\mathfrak{C}^{(3)},1\}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}_{1}\frac{(\log(ep))^{2}\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(en\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}_{2}\frac{(\log(ep))^{2/(q-2)}\sqrt{\log(pn)}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2/(q-2)}\sigma_{\min}}\log\left(en\right)

satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mu_{[1,n]}\leq\widetilde{\mu}_{1,n}{L}_{3,\max}+\widetilde{\mu}_{2,n}\nu_{q,\max}^{1/(q-2)},

which proves (
HYP-BE-1
) at
n
.
This proves our theorem.

4.4
For
4\leq q

In cases with finite fourth moment, we can obtain a better sample complexity by decomposing the third order remainder
\mathfrak{R}_{W_{j}}^{(3,1)}
. Based on the Taylor expansions up to order
4
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(3,1)}-\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(3,1)}\right]

(25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n]})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+2,n]})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}\otimes X_{j+1}\otimes(X_{j}+X_{j+1})]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+3,n]})\right],\mathbb{E}\left[X_{j}\otimes X_{j+1}\otimes X_{j+2}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(4,1)}-\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(4,1)}\right],

where
\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j}}^{(4,1)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{Y_{j}}^{(4,1)}
are remainder terms of the Taylor expansions specified in
Section

C.2
.
We re-apply the Lindeberg swapping. For brevity, we only look at the first term, but similar arguments apply to the other third order moment terms.
We observe that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbb{E}[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\right\rangle

(26)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})-\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\Big\rangle.

For the first term, because
Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)}
is Gaussian, Lemma 6.2 in
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2023
and Assumption (
VAR-EV
) imply that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{3/2}}L_{3,j}\frac{(\log(ep))^{3/2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{3}}

(27)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{3/2}}L_{3,j}\frac{(\log(ep))^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}\sigma_{\min}}.

For the second term, we re-apply the Lindeberg swapping: for
j=3,\dots,n-1
,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\Big\rangle.

(28)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{j-2}\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,k)}+X_{k}+Y_{(k,j-1)\cup(j+1,n)})\right]

\displaystyle{$- \mathbb{E}\left[ \nabla^3 \rho_{r,\phi}^\varepsilon(X_{[1,k)} + Y_k + Y_{(k,j-1) \cup(j+1,n)}) \right],
\mathbb{E}[ X_j^{\otimes 3} ]
\Big\rangle.$}

By the Taylor expansion cnetered at
X_{[1,k)}+Y_{(k,j-1)\cup(j+1,n]}
, the difference can be rewritten as a sum of sixth order remainder terms:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})-\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(Y_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n)})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\Big\rangle

(29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=3}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{j-2}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j},X_{k}}^{(6,1)}-\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j},Y_{k}}^{(6,1)}\right],

where
\mathfrak{R}_{X_{j},W_{k}}^{(6,1)}
is the sixth order remainder terms. The detail of the expansion and specification of the remainder term are given in
Section

C.4
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{3}\rho_{r,\phi}^{\varepsilon}(X_{[1,j-1)}+Y_{(j+1,n]})\right],\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{\otimes 3}]\right\rangle
